RTSM: Adapting Recursive Models to Multivariate Time-Series Classification

Georg Tenzing, Anna Tegon, Luca Benini, Thorir Mar Ingolfsson

cs.LG

Accurate multivariate time-series classification requires extracting temporal features and integrating information across a sequence. Weight-tied refinement offers a way to repeatedly process these features without allocating new parameters at each stage. Applying this principle to time series requires choosing both a temporal representation and a latent-state organization suited to classification. We introduce the Recursive Time-Series Model (RTSM), which combines a learned convolutional temporal tokenizer, single-state weight-tied refinement, and a pooled classification readout. Across all 30 UEA datasets, RTSM achieves 72.81% mean accuracy under validation-based configuration and checkpoint selection, the highest observed mean among the 17 evaluated deep-learning models and 1.60 percentage points above MambaSL. Ablations on eight datasets favor the proposed tokenizer over a simple convolutional embedding and the single-state model over the tested two-state variants. The complete classifier reaches 86.10% mean accuracy versus 84.79% for a separately trained tokenizer-only classifier. These findings identify temporal encoding and state organization as key design choices for adapting recursive architectures to time series. The resulting classifier combines competitive accuracy with a shared refinement module whose parameter count is independent of computational depth.

Forecasting Voltage Time Series under Anomalies Using a WGAN Model With Lipschitz Control and Progressive Kernel Critic Strategy

Najmieh Sadat Safarabadi

cs.LG

In time-series forecasting tasks, the use of recursive forecasting on high-dimensional data substantially increases the complexity of the prediction process. The main challenge is that long-term dynamics becomes harder to model, since the sequences show greater temporal variability. To counteract this limitation and improve the modeling of long-term dynamics, and more importantly, address high-dimensional recursive forecasting, this paper uses a Wasserstein GAN equipped with a progressive Lipschitz penalty and an adaptive kernel. This model is capable of taking a window of the data stream and forecasting the next values, subsequently reconstructing the time-series stream and dynamically adapting its kernel to each new window. The proopsed model is a deep learning framework based on a Wasserstein GAN (WGAN-CLP) with an adaptive kernel to model alterations in streaming voltage time series. To establish the methodological foundation, we review the WGAN-LP formulation another variant model in this class and extend its framework through an adaptive kernel critic model. We then evaluate this extended model using power ratios, correlation coefficient (CC), and relative root mean squared error (RRMSE). The experimental results demonstrate that the proposed model outperforms benchmark methods in both accuracy and robustness. The evaluation shows promising performance of the WGAN-CLP with an adaptive kernel framework in forecasting sequence alterations across the voltage and energy time-series datasets.

Contracting with an Agent under Model Mismatch

Elif Y{\i}lmaz, Jakub T{\l}uczek, Victor Villin, Christos Dimitrakakis

cs.LG, cs.GT

In the classical principal-agent setting, the principal delegates some task to the agent, who receives a payment conditioned on the outcome of the task, which in turn benefits the principal. To align their objectives, the principal designs a contract that incentivizes the agent to take actions beneficial to the principal. In practice, however, the agent may act under misspecified beliefs about the underlying environment, leading to systematically suboptimal decisions. In this work, we study this problem through a principal-agent Markov Decision Process (PAMDP), in which the principal repeatedly interacts with an agent whose transition model may differ from the true environment dynamics. The principal has access to the true transition model and designs contracts that account for the agent's belief and incentives. We characterize contract design under model mismatch and develop a framework that enables the principal to induce desirable long-term benefits even though the agent has a potentially inaccurate understanding of the environment.

Information Estimation with Discrete Diffusion

Alberto Foresti, Giulio Franzese, Pietro Michiardi

cs.LG, cs.IT, math.IT, https://proceedings.iclr.cc/paper_files/paper/2026/file/238747e153a84f50b43fd50fa8504f33-Paper-Conference.pdf, OpenReview:, https://openreview.net/forum?id=m18MXVdrV9., Code:, https://github.com/AlbertoForesti/mutinfo-diffusion

Information-theoretic measures, such as Mutual Information (MI), play a crucial role in understanding non-linear relationships between random variables and are widely used across scientific disciplines. Yet, their use on real-world discrete data remains challenging. Existing methods typically rely on embedding discrete data into a continuous space and apply neural estimators originally designed for continuous distributions. This process requires careful engineering for both the embedding model and estimator architecture, but suffers from issues related to high data dimensionality. In this work, we introduce InfoSEDD, a discrete diffusion-based approach that bridges information-theoretic estimation and generative modeling such that they can be used to compute Kullback-Leibler divergences. Backed by Continuous Time Markov Chains theory principles, the design of InfoSEDD is lightweight and scalable and allows seamless integration with pretrained models. We showcase the versatility of our approach through applications on motif discovery in genetic promoter data, semantic-aware model selection in text summarization, and entropy estimation in Ising models. Finally, we construct consistency tests on real-world textual and genomics data. Our experiments demonstrate that InfoSEDD outperforms alternatives that rely on the "embedding trick". Our results position InfoSEDD as a robust and scalable tool for information-theoretic analysis of discrete data.

Flash RNN-T: Memory-Efficient Joint Network and Transducer Loss Computation

Mahmoud Ashraf

cs.LG, cs.DC, cs.SD

The recurrent neural network transducer (RNN-T) remains a practical choice for streaming speech recognition, but training requires large intermediate tensors. We present Flash RNN-T, a joint and loss implementation combining specialized kernels with optional tiled recomputation over the full alignment space and vocabulary. Tiled and untiled Flash form the entire measured throughput-memory frontier on medium and long workloads. On H100, untiled Flash achieves 24-39% higher throughput with 26-56% less memory than the fastest baseline on these workloads wherever it fits. Tiling reduces memory further through recomputation. On medium, tiled Flash uses ninefold less memory than that baseline at 16% lower throughput when both use one backward call per batch. On long, it fits where all tested alternatives with that backward schedule run out of memory with 80 GB. These comparisons use each implementation's lowest-memory configuration reaching at least 95% of its peak throughput.

Residual Stochastic Concept Bottleneck Models

Stephen Marcou, Mateo Espinosa Zarlenga, Sonia Laguna, Moritz Vandenhirtz, Julia E. Vogt, Mateja Jamnik

cs.LG

Concept-based models (CMs) learn human-interpretable intermediate representations that enable users to understand the model's reasoning and steer its predictions through test-time concept interventions. However, in realistic settings, their performance can be limited by concept incompleteness, where the training concepts fail to capture all task-relevant information. Adding an unconstrained residual pathway to the model's predictive process can overcome the consequences of incompleteness. However, this introduces an inscrutable, leak-prone prediction pathway that may weaken concept interventions, as these often leave residual variables unchanged. To address this limitation, we introduce Residual Stochastic Concept Bottleneck Models (Res-SCBMs), a family of concept-based models that jointly model concept and residual variables through a multivariate Gaussian distribution. Res-SCBMs learn a covariance structure that explicitly captures dependencies between the known concepts and learnt residual variables, allowing interventions to propagate to statistically dependent residual variables through Gaussian conditioning. This extends the intervention capabilities of previous CMs without requiring concept completeness during training. Our results show that, across multiple concept-incomplete benchmarks, Res-SCBMs improve predictive performance over competing baselines while retaining effective test-time concept interventions.

Bridging Compressed Communication and Local Steps via Spectral Method in Federated Learning

Dmitriy Bystrov, George Chirkov, Daniil Medyakov, Dmitry Bylinkin, Aleksandr Beznosikov

cs.LG

Federated Learning (FL) enables collaborative training of complex models across large-scale networks of edge devices while keeping data localized. However, data heterogeneity and high communication overhead remain fundamental bottlenecks. Recently, the spectral optimizer Muon has demonstrated remarkable advantages over Adam-like methods in centralized training. While recent efforts have attempted to transition Muon to FL, naive integrations into traditional architectures fail short. Crucially, existing approaches do not support the the communication compression and local steps --- the two foundational techniques of efficient FL. To address this limitation, we introduce a novel spectral FL method that utilizes both local updates and compression. We establish rigorous convergence guarantees under mild assumptions, notably eliminating the restrictive bounded heterogeneity condition. Empirical evaluation on standard image classification benchmarks validates the efficiency and robustness of our approach.

RECREG: Recovery-based Shielding for Regular Safety Properties in Reinforcement Learning

Alexander W. Goodall, Francesco Belardinelli

cs.LG, cs.AI

In many real-world scenarios we need to consider formal requirements, such as safety and constraint compliance, to deploy Reinforcement Learning (RL) agents. In this paper we introduce RecReg, a safe RL framework capable of enforcing non-Markovian regular safety properties via recovery-based shielding. RecReg is a unified approach that supports different levels of a priori knowledge and safety guarantees. The paper primarily focuses on building the theoretical foundations for RecReg in the tabular setting, providing rigorous safety and convergence guarantees that are not present in related work. We provide an extensive set of empirical results using the MASA-Safe-RL library, and we outline an extension of RecReg to deep RL domains using SOTA model-based RL. Our empirical results demonstrate that RecReg achieves efficient learning and matches the theory in this paper.

Regularized free deconvolution for spectral repair of resampling distortion in high-dimensional particle filters

Deming Song, Chi-Kin Lam, Kefeng Li, Hui Yu, Xilin Li, Huagang Lin

cs.LG, (Elsevier)., The, implementation, and, experiment, scripts, are, available, at, https://github.com/songming-teaching/spectral-repair-resampling., This, is, the, second, paper, in, a, series, on, spectral, analysis, of, particle, filters., The, first, paper, is, available, at, arXiv:2610.16680

Resampling is the standard remedy for weight degeneracy in particle filters, but in high dimensions it distorts the spectrum of the ensemble covariance in a way that is computable from the importance weights. This paper studies the inverse problem: recovering the pre-resampling spectrum from the observed weights and the empirical spectrum alone, and using it without biasing the filter. We first show that the forward spectral law extends to random importance weights once one conditions on the weights together with the realized offspring counts, rather than on the weights alone. We then construct a regularized free-deconvolution estimator, prove its consistency with an explicit error budget at a fixed resolution scale, and certify model mismatch through a computable forward residual. The repaired spectrum enters the filter only as the preconditioner of a Metropolis-adjusted rejuvenation move, so that spectral error affects mixing efficiency but not the invariant target. For the full filtering cycle, we derive a geometrically summable error recursion under stable scalar dynamics and an explicit law for the cost of abstaining from repair. Pre-registered simulations show that the inversion reduces the spectral error by an order of magnitude, that the corrected preconditioner recovers about 90% of the oracle's sampling efficiency in an anisotropic regime, and that the cost of abstention depends on when abstentions occur rather than on how many. The repair recovers eigenvalue laws, not eigenvectors, and is certified only within an explicitly stated exogenous-weight regime.

Predictable Scaling: Efficient Model Exploration via Scaling-Law Prediction

Aishwarya Wesanekar, Sheng-Tao Yang, Jiawei Zhang, Flavio Sales Truzzi, Chen Chen, Nader Beigiparast, Shengyuan Huang, Roshanak Zilouchian Moghaddam, Vitor Cid, Colin Clement, Vinayak Thapliyal, Sebastian Ament, Jihao Andreas Lin, Vincent Huang, Kai Han, Boyi Jiang, Wenlin Chen, Chaorong Chen, Ashwin Kumar, Maximilian Balandat, David Eriksson, Yifei Zhang, Pan Zhong

cs.LG

Evaluating candidate changes (hypotheses) to recommendation models requires full-scale training runs consuming thousands of GPU-hours, severely limiting the hypothesis space engineers can feasibly explore. We introduce Predictable Scaling (PS), a systematic framework that exploits empirically verified regularity conditions to predict full-scale model performance from small-scale proxies. PS constructs scaled-down variants via composable scaling functions along distinct resource axes, and projects metrics to the target scale via curve fitting with calibrated uncertainty estimates. We compare three prediction strategies of increasing expressiveness: even the simplest (raw proxy) achieves prediction error within the empirical noise floor of repeated-run variance; the most expressive (Gaussian Process) provides an indicative 15% relative MAE improvement and calibrated uncertainty. Evaluation on two ranking models of increasing complexity---a task-specific model and a foundation model---demonstrates that PS correctly ranks actionable hypotheses across diverse hypothesis types at 79-88% compute reductions.

A Topological Signature of Learning under Noisy Labels

Zhongtian Sun, Fan Mo, Prayag Tiwari, Kelin Xia

cs.LG, cs.AI

How does noisy supervision change the representations learned by a neural network? Training accuracy measures agreement with training labels but provides little information about representation geometry. We use persistent homology to track loop structure across distance thresholds in neural activations. Experiments span image classification, text classification, and modular arithmetic. On a fixed CIFAR-10/ResNet-18 training probe, equally sized clean-label and corrupted-label subsets show no detectable persistence difference at epoch 10, whereas corrupted-label subsets have higher persistence at epoch 50. The contrast survives uniform-scale controls, even though predictions match the assigned noisy labels for only 7-11% of corrupted probe examples at that checkpoint. Normalisation preserves the crossover between clean and noisy training trajectories on CIFAR-10/ResNet-18, whereas CIFAR-100/ResNet-18 shows no crossover under any of the five evaluated constructions. In a separate noise-rate calibration comparison across five pipelines, mean activation norm achieves a higher mean in-sample R2 than raw persistence (0.936 versus 0.908). These findings show that noisy supervision leaves a measurable geometric signature even when agreement with corrupted labels is low. Comparing persistence under scale controls with label-fitting measurements offers a way to study how representations respond to label corruption beyond what training accuracy reveals.

Uncertainty-aware AI-enabled framework for predicting hydrogen dispersion in confined spaces to enhance operator situational awareness

Niclas Flehmig, Mary Ann Lundteigen, Shen Yin

cs.LG

We present a two-stage AI-enabled framework for enhancing operator situational awareness during compressed hydrogen bunkering in maritime confined spaces. In the event of a leak, the framework first estimates the unknown leakage rate from early sensor readings and then forecasts how the hydrogen cloud will disperse over the next time steps, providing operators with a predicted concentration map together with confidence bounds. The framework is based on two independent AI models building one AI system. The AI system is designed as a decision-support tool. It augments sensor readings, and thus enhances operator situational awareness and supports decision-making. This enables preventive safety barriers to be deployed before hazardous concentrations are reached, while an independent emergency shutdown system remains the ultimate safety layer. The system design incorporates safeguards to ensure the AI system operates within its competence envelope. Trained and tested on an open dataset of confined-space hydrogen releases, the framework achieves a mean absolute error of $0.052$~g/s in leakage-rate estimation and $\approx0.018$ in hydrogen-concentration prediction, approaching the sensor precision bound of $\pm0.01$. The hydrogen-concentration prediction provides well-calibrated uncertainty estimates that allow operators to judge the reliability of each forecast. These results suggest that the framework may provide early information about hazardous concentration build-up, potentially supporting preventive actions by operators.

Out of Support, Not Out of Reach: In-Context Learning with Unsupported Instructions and Composed Tasks

Xuhan Tong, Yuchen Zeng, Jiawei Zhang

cs.LG

In-Context Learning (ICL) enables pretrained LLMs to adapt to downstream tasks from a small set of input-output demonstrations, without parameter updates. Yet existing theory primarily explains ICL as identifying a latent task already represented in pretraining, leaving unclear whether ICL can succeed when either the instruction or the target task falls outside the pretraining support. We develop a theoretical framework under mild assumptions that separates these two forms of distribution shift. For target tasks represented in pretraining, we show that demonstrations can identify the task and reduce sensitivity to pretrain-supported prompt templates as their number increases. For an unsupported instruction, we show that under a fixed model-induced reference extension, demonstration evidence can dominate an $N$-independent instruction shift while preserving the exponential identification rate. We then use the same ICL framework as an interpretive lens for long-horizon target tasks that are not represented in pretraining but admit a plan that decomposes them into pretraining-supported subtasks. Applying the supported-task analysis step by step yields a standard accumulated-error bound and clarifies how invoking familiar subtasks along an intermediate-state trajectory can avoid directly selecting the unsupported complete task. Together, our results characterize when demonstrations, instructions, and task decomposition can support ICL beyond the corresponding pretraining supports.

Beyond Expected Values: Risk-Sensitive Planning with Distributional Monte-Carlo Tree Search

Quynh Trinh, Trung Nguyen, Nam Nguyen, Tuan Dam

cs.LG, stat.ML, Processing, Systems

Monte-Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) typically stores only scalar mean estimates, which makes it poorly suited for tail-risk objectives such as conditional value-at-risk (CVaR). We introduce Categorical Thompson Sampling with Optimism (CATSO) and Particle Thompson Sampling with Optimism (PATSO), two distributional MCTS algorithms that maintain finite-support empirical backup laws at Q-edges. CATSO represents Q-edge laws with categorical atoms and Dirichlet Thompson sampling, while PATSO represents them with adaptive particles. Both algorithms select actions using the CVaR of a Thompson-sampled Q-edge law plus a polynomial optimism bonus, and propagate scalar continuation values through visit-weighted averages of child Q-edge CVaR scores. For the finite-depth nested CVaR planning target, we prove an $O(n^{-1/2})$ bound on the root value error after $n$ rollouts, which upper-bounds the risk-sensitive simple regret. CATSO adds a fixed-grid discretization bias, while capped PATSO adds a tunable Wasserstein compression bias. Experiments on stochastic planning benchmarks show that distributional Q-edges improve lower-tail return and reduce catastrophic outcomes when mean-optimal and risk-sensitive behavior differ.

Boundary Detection for High-Dimensional Point Clouds

Abigail Hickok, Valerio Galanti, Osama Al Dalahmah, Andrew J. Blumberg

cs.LG, cs.CG, stat.ML

We introduce two new methods for boundary detection in high-dimensional point cloud data, intended to work in situations with non-uniform density, high ambient dimension, and substantial noise. Our first method fits a separating hyperplane on local neighborhoods after doing local dimension reduction---this method works best when one can reliably estimate the intrinsic dimension of the data. Our second method measures the directional uniformity of local neighborhoods, and has the best overall performance on synthetic data. In addition to pointwise numerical boundary scores, our algorithms yield an estimated normal vector at each boundary point. We validate our methodology on synthetic data as well as two real examples: we analyze spatial transcriptomics data, where we show that we can detect the boundary of a tumor, and we also study LLM activation geometry.

When Magnitude Suffices: Reconstructing Time-Domain-Gated Microwave Signals Without Phase on a Thoracic Phantom

Maciej Cicho\'n, Patrycja Go\l{}a\'s, Maksymilian Bartosik, Angelika Walczak, Agata Bielecka-D\k{a}browa, Maciej \'Slot

cs.LG, physics.app-ph

Time-domain gating separates the arrivals that form a microwave S-parameter measurement. It requires the measurement of complex values, so this application almost always uses a vector network analyzer (VNA). We show that, on one calibrated measurement configuration, the gated magnitude the VNA would give can be reconstructed from a magnitude-only sweep of a spectrum analyzer with a tracking generator (SA). We select the gate center for separability of the measured states, correct each instrument once by a per-bin subtraction of a reference state, and train a fully connected network on sweeps from both instruments. The test object is a torso phantom with lung-shaped compartments whose filling takes eleven dielectric states, each held out in turn. On $S_{11}$ the reconstruction from SA input reaches a shape error of 0.08 dB$^2$ with a state ordering of 0.81. This matches the reconstruction from VNA input. On $S_{21}$ it reaches 0.82 dB$^2$ and keeps the ordered groups of the states. An explicit phase prediction performs much worse, because gating needs the phase to about 0.2 rad RMS. Across eleven measurement configurations the dependence of phase on magnitude is strong for reflection and weak for transmission. This explains the difference between $S_{11}$ and $S_{21}$. Where the magnitude carries enough of the phase, a scalar instrument gives the gated response without any phase measurement after installation. On the open UM-BMID breast data set, with whole phantoms held out, the shape error of the network is 30 percent lower than that of a per-bin linear map.

How Many, but How Sure? Class Competition as a Confidence Proxy in Multi-Class Density-Based Counting

Villanelle O'Reilly, Georgios Leontidis, Marc Hanheide

cs.LG, abstract

Multi-class density estimators can count objects in crowded scenes, but their class-wise outputs offer no indication of how reliably density mass has been allocated. We introduce a training-free confidence proxy based on local competition between the strongest class channels. The resulting prominence measure requires only the predicted density maps, with no retraining or access to model internals. We test whether residual competition is diagnostic of cross-class leakage by attenuating contested density at inference time. On DSACA predictions for the Hicks et al. dataset, this reduces overall MAE from 0.920 to 0.846, suggesting that class competition contains useful information about count reliability.

The Sign Code: The Hidden Binary Nature of Deep Networks

Niclas G\"oring, Shuofeng Zhang, Roi Holtzman, Yoonsoo Nam, Ard A. Louis

cs.LG

Every neuron in a trained network outputs a number that can be separated into its sign (positive or negative) and its magnitude (absolute size). We call the set of all neuron signs the \textit{sign code}. This raises a key question: does classification depend more on the sign code or on the magnitudes? For overparameterized vision classifiers with sign-preserving activations, the answer is overwhelmingly the sign code. A $k$-nearest neighbor classifier operating only on this binary sign code achieves accuracy comparable to the full model across different architectures, activation functions, and datasets. Moreover, replacing all magnitudes in every layer with random noise while keeping the original signs barely changes residual network performance. In contrast, flipping just 20\% of the signs reduces accuracy to chance. Models generalize well when same-class inputs share similar sign codes. When this overlap is disrupted by a regularizer, training accuracy remains high, but test performance collapses. A margin decomposition helps explain these results: the sign-only contribution to each logit grows as $\sqrt{d}$ in network width, dominating the magnitude residual. The natural unit of analysis for these networks may not be through their continuous weight space but instead through their discrete sign codes.

ThetreStageAI: Multi-View Retrieval-Augmented Generation for Structured Bengali Theatre Production Planning

Md. Mehedi Hasan Naeem, Mst. Kamrunnahar Ruma, Shakila Sultana, Nafiza Anjum, Nasreen Jannatun Nemu, Md. Sujan Ali

cs.LG, Conference, on, Sustainable, Technologies, and, Engineering, (IEEE, iCOSTE, 2026),, Cox's, Bazar,, Bangladesh,, November, 1-3,, 2026

Staging a theatre performance requires coordinating dialogue, actor movement, stage directions, lighting focus, cue timing, and equipment constraints within the same production plan. This work presents ThetreStageAI, a retrieval augmented framework for structured Bengali theatre production planning. Rather than retrieving from one mixed corpus, the system maintains separate scene, actor-blocking, and lighting views. Bengali text is embedded with BGE-M3 and indexed in view-specific Qdrant collections. Retrieved references are combined with a production brief and passed to Gemini, which generates schema-constrained JSON containing characters, scenes, dialogue, stage directions, blocking, lighting, and optional sound cues. Structural and semantic checks are applied before acceptance, with at most one constrained repair attempt. We also construct a human-guided, LLM-assisted synthetic dataset of 500 short Bengali theatre records that expands to 1,500 retrieval documents. Dataset diagnostics confirm consistent identifiers and valid lighting fields while also revealing substantial dialogue repetition. A paired camera-ready evaluation compares single-combined and full multi-view retrieval under matched settings. The source code, dataset, and evaluation materials are publicly available.

GRAFT-EEG: Gauge-Aware Physical Field Transport for Cross-Dataset Motor-Imagery EEG

Ying Zhou, Mengting Liu

cs.LG, eess.SP

Cross-dataset EEG decoding often treats montage, reference, and active-sensor changes as representation shift, although they also alter the observation operator. Deconstructing Multi-Task Active Learning: The Paradox of Gradient Conflict and Orthogonal Decomposition

Juho Jung, Seongjae Kang, Jinyoung Han

cs.LG

Existing multi-task active learning (MTL-AL) methods predominantly rely on linear scalarization of heterogeneous objectives such as uncertainty, diversity, and gradient conflict. We argue that this entangled formulation is fundamentally limited, as linear combination fails to capture Pareto-optimal trade-offs under conflicting objectives. We propose a shift from combination to deconstruction and introduce a principled framework that rethinks MTL-AL along three orthogonal dimensions. First, we identify the Conflict Paradox: although the optimization literature treats gradient conflict as interference to be suppressed, penalizing conflict when ranking candidates is measurably worse than not scoring it at all. We therefore decouple the two concerns: the acquisition function does not score conflict, and the resulting interference is resolved by gradient surgery during training. Second, we introduce Orthogonal Decomposition, separating acquisition into Informativeness and Exploration. This two-stage process, informativeness filtering followed by coverage maximization, prevents "diversity dilution" by excluding uninformative outliers before enforcing diversity. Third, we propose Cross-Task Mutual Information (CTMI), a covariance-based statistic of cross-task dependency computed from MC-dropout task entropies. Extensive experiments on COCO, Cityscapes, and NYUv2, including comparisons against recent MTL-AL and data-selection baselines, demonstrate consistent improvements over state-of-the-art methods, highlighting that structural rethinking of acquisition yields larger gains than incremental heuristic design. Code is available at https://github.com/Juho-Jung/multitask_active_learning_official.

Deep LLM layers expose passage-level context in EEG during silent reading

Ingo Marquardt, Anthilia Alchanat, Priyanka Jain

cs.LG, q-bio.NC, (including, 5, in, Supplementary, Material)

In a previous report we showed that a decoder can recover open-vocabulary word-level information from 19-channel EEG recorded during silent reading, by aligning short EEG windows with hidden-state embeddings of the presented word taken from a large language model (LLM). That analysis contrasted two of the model's 33 layers: the context-free input embedding and one mid-depth layer. Here we ask how EEG-decodable structure is distributed over the full depth of the language model. We trained one decoder per layer of Llama-3.1-8B, with and without a causal transformer over the sequence of EEG windows. We distinguish word-level decodability from a context-tracking component. Word-level decodability peaks in early layers and declines steadily towards the last layer. In contrast, the context-tracking component of the retrieval gain rises with depth, both with and without a causal transformer. Unexpectedly, a decoder that sees a single 0.5-s window at a time tracks the text passage too. Two new controls dissect this component. A target-geometry control, a synthetic predictor made of the true target plus noise calibrated to each decoder's word-level accuracy, shows that context tracking is not a by-product of word decoding and target geometry. A control that permutes predictions across runs from either the same or from different sessions shows that most of the observed context-tracking is not caused by an EEG recording session signature. Instead, context tracking mostly follows the text. A single EEG window therefore carries passage-level information that a contextual target can absorb. The results locate word identity in the early layers of the language model, discourse state in the deeper layers, and make the choice of target layer a design dial for EEG language decoders.

No-Regret Language Generation

Jiaxun Li, Vinod Raman, Ambuj Tewari

cs.LG, stat.ML

We introduce no-regret generation, which requires a generator to make a sublinear number of mistakes uniformly over all demonstration sequences and compatible target languages. JAXtari: High-Throughput and Easy-to-Modify Arcade Learning Environment

Quentin Delfosse, Raban Emunds, Paul Seitz, Sebastian Wette, Jannis Bl\"uml, Daniel Kirn, Dominik Mandok, Kristian Kersting

cs.LG

The Arcade Learning Environment (ALE) has been a cornerstone of Deep Reinforcement Learning research, providing a standardized benchmark for evaluating general agents. However, its lack of native support for object-extraction and environment modification restricts research into evaluating whether agents learn robust causal models or rely on brittle visual heuristics. Furthermore, the ALE's dependence on CPU-based emulation creates a significant bottleneck in modern GPU-accelerated training pipelines, limiting both experimental scale and iteration frequency. In this work, we introduce JAXtari, an open-source, high-performance reimplementation of Atari 2600 environments. TriRouter: Sparse-to-Dense Electrode-Space Routing for Robust EEG Motor Imagery Decoding

Ying Zhou, Yaoxin, Mengting Liu

cs.LG, eess.SP

Motor imagery brain-computer interfaces require reliable decoding from multichannel EEG, but practical acquisition often yields sparse or corrupted electrode montages. Equivalence testing and run-count design for stochastic machine-learning experiments

Dmytro Knopov, Nadiya Shvai

cs.LG, stat.ML, case-study, data:, https://github.com/DmytroKnopov/equivtest, (release, v0.2.0,, archived, at, https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23421510)

Claims that two training methods perform equally well often rest on a non-significant difference between two or three runs per method. We develop a protocol, with its power theory, for certifying practical equivalence in per-run metrics such as the AUROC (area under the receiver operating characteristic curve). It rests on a two-level (seed x item) variance model separating between-run dispersion from item-level noise. Equivalence within a pre-specified margin is tested by two one-sided tests (TOST), equivalently by a confidence interval inside the margin; a two-level bootstrap covers composite statistics. A closed-form formula converts a margin and a target power into runs per method; its shifted-t refinement is calibrated against the realised two-level process. Simulations on a 270-cell grid and under non-Gaussian seed laws confirm the formula within its design-relevant range, from five runs per method. For four published runs of a joint energy-based model, the failure to certify equivalence was largely decided by design: with the variance calibrated to those runs, two per method certify with probability near three per cent, whereas certifying 0.01 AUROC at 80 per cent power needs twelve even under exact equality. A second case study on a thousand-seed CIFAR-10 zoo and the MultiBERTs checks the formula against real seed laws (within 0.05 in that range) and resolves comparisons that two or three runs leave undecided. In a two-stage design (pilot, then formula), our experiments suggest that an upper confidence bound on the pilot variance substantially reduces the undersizing seen with its point estimate.

Blitz: Accelerating the Diffusion Modules of AlphaFold3 and Boltz-2

Vinay Saji Mathew, Soundar Kumara, Gretta Kellogg, William K. M. Lai

cs.LG

Diffusion-based protein structure prediction models produce all-atom structures in about 200 denoising steps, and that cost has driven a number of few-step samplers. We present Blitz, a few-step distillation framework that uses a geometric noise schedule to distribute steps more evenly across noise scales, then learns those updates through multistep consistency training. Physical steering and target-free candidate selection help reduce the remaining geometric defects at inference. We apply Blitz to AlphaFold 3 and Boltz-2, reducing denoising from 200 steps to eight. Because published few-step results use different benchmarks and scoring procedures, we compare both distilled models with their teachers and other few-step methods under a common evaluation protocol covering structural accuracy, interface quality, ligand placement, and stereochemical validity. Blitz retains teacher-level structural accuracy, with AF3-Blitz outperforming all evaluated few-step baselines and exceeding its teacher on several accuracy metrics.

Tool Recommendation for Multi-Step Agents: Finding Every Tool a Task Needs

Sam Partee, River Stevens, Thierry Damiba, Valerie Fanelle

cs.LG, cs.IR

A multi-step agent task fails when a single tool it needs is missing, yet the tools an agent may need are too many to list: the roughly 8,200 tools on the platform we study have definitions that together exceed the context window of any model we evaluate. We present Tool Recommendation, a deployed system that gives an agent all of them, hundreds of times more than a typical MCP server exposes, through a fixed interface of three tools. It runs at the gateway between agent and tools, executing every call under the end user's own credentials and checking it against the user's consent for each service and the tools the agent may use. We evaluate it on both a public retrieval benchmark and billed end-to-end session cost. On ToolRet, Tool Recommendation places every tool a query needs in the top ten 57% more often than BM25, a lexical ranking that commercial tool search offers, and compares favorably with published systems up to nine times its size. In paired multi-step workflows, its first recommendation covered every required tool in 93% of Codex sessions, and against a fixed list of 100 tools it cut input tokens by 17% with Codex and 40% with Claude Code and the total bill in all 14 runs, by up to 40%, with no change in task completion.

Neural Expansion: A Unified Mechanism for How Deep Neural Networks Generalize

Chashi Mahiul Islam, Samuel Jacob Chacko, Mao Nishino, Canlin Zhang, Xiuwen Liu

cs.LG, cs.AI, https://github.com/meetchashi/neural_expansion_neurips_2026

Deep neural networks demonstrate remarkable generalization despite being highly over-parameterized, challenging traditional statistical learning theories. While several theoretical paradigms such as margin-based bounds, PAC-Bayesian analyses, and neural tangent kernels provide global explanations, they often lack mechanistic insight into how generalization emerges from training dynamics. We introduce Neural Expansion, a unified geometric framework that characterizes generalization through the expansion of directionally-selective ray coverage in the input space. Using Generalization Intervals (GIs), we quantify direction-specific regions around training samples where the model's predictions remain stable. Our extensive empirical studies across diverse architectures and datasets reveal that training leads to progressive expansion of ray coverage, particularly along directions with low Jacobian singular values. This mechanism helps explain a large fraction of correctly classified test inputs, adversarial examples, and even mislabeled or out-of-distribution samples through the geometry of ray coverage rather than instance-level memorization. By connecting input-space curvature to predictive behavior, our framework provides a mechanistic foundation that complements existing global theories of deep learning.

Escaping Parameter Space: Tight Generalization Bounds via Representation Quality

Niclas G\"oring, Shuofeng Zhang, Branton DeMoss, Ard A. Louis

cs.LG

We derive a tight generalization bound for deterministic neural We derive a tight generalization bound for deterministic neural networks based on their last hidden layer representations. By evaluating representation quality with a parameter-free $k$-nearest-neighbor ($k$-NN) classifier, our certificate avoids traditional parameter-space complexity measures, KL divergences to a posterior, weight norms, and compression. The bound is tight: trained from scratch, it reaches an 8.0 percentage point gap to test error on CIFAR-10 (ResNet-18) and yields the first non-vacuous certificates we are aware of on CIFAR-100; with frozen DINOv2 representations, it certifies at 1.4\% on CIFAR-10 and 23.1\% on 1,000-class ImageNet. It decomposes additively into three empirical, independently measurable quantities: local class geometry ($k$-NN decoder error), decoder-network agreement, and training stability. This allows the bound to act as a diagnostic tool: we demonstrate that actively destroying local class geometry eliminates generalization while leaving dataset memorization intact, identifying class geometry as a causally relevant mechanism for generalization. Finally, we show that learned representations are significantly less sensitive to weight noise than linear readouts, suggesting why parameter-space certificates often struggle to remain tight.

LagNet: Lag-Structured Operators for Long-Term Time Series Forecasting

Zhenan Yu, Guangxin Jiang, Jiuyun Jiang, Jin Yang

cs.LG

Long-term time series forecasting maps a finite history window to an extended future horizon. Many existing models focus primarily on the design of input representations and model structures, while paying less attention to the structural form of the history-to-future forecasting operator itself. We examine this operator directly and find that, across model families, sensitivity matrices exhibit recurring diagonal patterns whose diagonals correspond to relative temporal lags. Motivated by this observation, we develop a framework for direct multi-step forecasting based on lag-structured temporal operators. Its basic building block, LagLayer, constructs a Toeplitz matrix by tying history-to-future connections with the same relative lag and incorporates horizon-wise and history-wise scaling coefficients to relax exact sharing. This design enables LagLayer to serve as an effective replacement for temporal linear layers in existing forecasting models. Stacking LagLayer modules with activations and multiple feature maps yields LagNet. Experiments on long-term time series forecasting benchmarks show that our method is competitive with strong baselines, while sensitivity matrix diagnostics and limited-data experiments demonstrate that LagLayer provides an effective and sample-efficient alternative to a linear layer.

Universal Semantic Enhancement for Weakly Supervised Anomaly Detection

Siyu Zhou, Yuqin Xie, Hao Yuan, Yingjie Zhou

cs.LG

Weakly supervised anomaly detection on tabular data is an essential task in many label-scarce applications, since tabular data is widely encountered and it is commonly observed in practice that only a limited number of labeled anomalies are available alongside abundant unlabeled samples. Most existing methods try to learn features that capture the inherent distribution patterns in the target domain. However, such features often struggle to provide a complete description of possible abnormal characteristics and are highly susceptible to overfitting the extremely scarce labels. To address this challenge, we propose a weakly supervised anomaly detection method based on universal semantic enhancement for tabular data, which fuses the universal knowledge from a pre-trained tabular foundation model with the domain-specific knowledge learned on the target dataset. The two types of knowledge are naturally complementary: universal semantics describe the common patterns of tabular data across domains, while the domain-specific features capture the inherent distributions of normal and anomalous behaviors in the target dataset. A prototype-conditioned modulation mechanism is then designed to effectively integrate the two types of features, allowing them to reinforce each other for improved anomaly detection. Extensive experiments on public datasets show that the proposed method outperforms competitive methods when only eight labeled anomalies are available in the training procedure.

Logic-Guided Graph Denoising Diffusion Models

Samuel Cognolato, Luciano Serafini, Alessandro Sperduti, Andrea Cavallaro

cs.LG

Graph diffusion models are competitive approaches for generating complex graph-structured data. However, when such distributions have strict structural constraints, diffusion models may fail to capture them exactly, resulting in the generation of invalid graphs. In this paper, we propose a method for grounding graph diffusion models in domain-specific knowledge via First Order Logic (FOL), leveraging its expressive and interpretable formalism to impose structural constraints on the generative process. We incorporate domain knowledge using classifier guidance, a technique that conditions the diffusion model's distribution on external variables to exert control over the sampled data. During the generative process, classifier guidance steers the denoising trajectory to optimize an objective function, which we define as a fuzzy and differentiable relaxation of the logical rules. Our methodology requires only the specification of constraints in FOL, whose satisfaction is encouraged at inference time to guide a pre-trained generative model. We evaluate our approach by comparing the unconditional generation of knowledge graphs with our logic-guided framework, which incorporates domain ontologies as structural constraints during inference. This approach significantly increases the proportion of generated graphs that satisfy the ontological constraints.

Explainable Machine Learning Analysis of Temperature Effects on Mechanical Strength of Fiber-Reinforced High-Performance Concrete

Jagaran Chakma, Zhiguang Zhou, Badhan Chakma, Priyanta Chakma

cs.LG

Predicting the compressive strength of fiber-reinforced high-performance concrete subjected to temperature exposure is challenging because to the intricate interdependencies of mixture composition, fiber properties, curing conditions, and temperature. A data-driven framework to predict compressive strength utilizing 1,204 experimental records containing a diverse array of material designs and exposure situations. This study evaluates eight machine-learning models and demonstrate that ensemble approaches routinely surpass individual learners. Both gradient boosting and extreme gradient boosting exhibit superior performance on unseen data, each attaining a R^2 of 0.903. Gradient boosting produces the minimal root mean square error (6.67 MPa), whereas extreme gradient boosting achieves the lowest mean absolute error (3.97 MPa) and mean absolute percentage error (10.60%). The models are further interpreted utilizing Shapley additive explanations and partial dependence analysis, which reveal temperature as the primary factor influencing compressive strength, succeeded by fine aggregate, superplasticizer, cement content, water-to-binder ratio, and water content. Strength diminishes significantly with rising temperature, albeit the extent of deterioration is contingent upon the specific combination design. The best performing models are subsequently implemented in a Python-based graphical user interface for expedited prediction. Our findings offer a precise and comprehensible methodology for evaluating temperature-dependent compressive strength and facilitate the practical, data-driven design of fiber-reinforced high-performance concrete.

Learning Personalized Memory Gating from Delayed One-Sided Feedback

Kaixuan Ji, Zixiang Chen, Wenting Zhao, Cheng Qian, Heng Wang, Weizhi Zhang, Jielin Qiu, Liangwei Yang, Quanquan Gu, Silvio Savarese, Shelby Heinecke, Huan Wang

cs.LG

Long-term memory enables language-model agents to personalize future interactions, but a finite memory bank cannot preserve every extracted fact. Existing memory gating policies are typically shared across users and therefore fail to account for user-specific patterns of future reuse. In this paper, we study memory gating at the fact level. We first propose a retrieval-based labeling strategy that labels each fact by whether it is later retrieved and incorporated into responses. We then formulate online memory gating as contextual apple tasting with delayed one-sided feedback, and propose Warm-Start UCB, which combines offline learning from existing data with online adaptation to the current user. Our theoretical analysis establishes a high-probability regret upper bound of $\tilde{O}(T\epsilon \wedge d\sqrt{T})$ when the delay window is at most $O(\sqrt{T})$, where $T$ is the total number of rounds, $\epsilon$ is the initialization error, $d$ is the dimension of the feature map, and $\tilde{O}(\cdot)$ hides constant and poly-logarithmic factors. The results of our experiment demonstrate that our method outperforms all baselines under different memory budgets and history lengths, establishing it as a promising personalized memory gating policy.

CocoRNA: Collective RNA Design with Cooperative Multi-agent Reinforcement Learning

Tianmeng Hu, Biao Luo, Ke Li

cs.LG, cs.MA

Designing RNA sequences that reliably fold into specific secondary structures is essential for understanding their biological functions but remains a challenging computational problem. We propose CocoRNA, a cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning framework for RNA inverse design. CocoRNA simplifies the design task by decomposing it into smaller sub-problems, each solved collaboratively by multiple agents. This approach reduces the complexity of the problem and improves the exploration of design policies. During training, a centralized critic uses global structural information to guide the agents, enabling them to jointly optimize their design strategies. As a result, CocoRNA learns high-quality RNA design policies that generalize effectively to unseen structures without additional training. Experiments on the Rfam dataset demonstrate that CocoRNA substantially outperforms state-of-the-art methods in both success rate and design speed. Further experiments on other biological sequence design tasks highlight the effectiveness and broad potential of CocoRNA for complex design tasks.

Waiting Buys Exactly Two Moments: Endogenous Identification in Prediction-Driven Allocation

Rama AlHamidi, Rasul Khanbayov, Erchin Serpedin, Hasan Kurban

cs.LG

A planner with a fixed budget of preventive help must decide whom to help and when. Waiting reveals risk but removes intervention opportunities as higher-risk individuals leave first. We characterize this tradeoff under repeated noisy signals and risk-dependent attrition: in the canonical model, a length-$\tau$ observation prefix identifies exactly the latent risk moments $m_j=\mathbb E[p^j]$ for $j=0,\ldots,2\tau-1$, buying two new moments per additional period. These identify average next-period attrition among survivors but need not identify allocation welfare or attrition among those prioritized for treatment. Our observationally equivalent constructions establish unavoidable timing regret at any sample size for a fixed observation length, while exact certificates determine whether all compatible populations share a unique optimal date. Matched experiments separate the effects of estimation, bootstrap averaging, and regret weighting. Longer observation often resolves timing ambiguity, but the optimal date may already have passed. In our experiments, bootstrap averaging and early stopping improve decisions, while regret weighting adds no improvement in pooled mean squared regret: better statistical fit need not yield better allocation welfare. The code and artifacts are available at https://github.com/KurbanIntelligenceLab/waiting-buys-two-moments.

Towards Adaptive and Real-Survey-Aligned Simulated Urban Mobility Behaviour of Generative Agents

Yves Bru, Kamaldeep Singh Oberoi, Benoit Gaudou

cs.MA, cs.AI

In existing agent-based simulations of multimodal urban mobility, agents, representing humans, choose their travel modes either using discrete choice or machine learning models fitted on structured travel survey data, or with rules explicitly written by the domain experts. Such approaches fix a priori the agents' possible behaviour space. Hence, a factor absent from variables or rules cannot affect agents' decisions. In reality, choosing a transport mode is a multidimensional decision in which objective and subjective factors intertwine and change over time. Integrating such complexity into a city-scale multimodal mobility simulation remains a challenge. Generative agents (GAs) built on large language models promise to respond to this challenge because they carry decision heuristics that travel surveys do not record. However, to the best of our knowledge, aligning their decisions with real household travel surveys remains an open question. In this paper, we build an agent-based model of daily multimodal mobility for Toulouse (France). We compare the performance of fifteen decision-makers (simple baselines, models fitted on the survey, GAs with minimal and tuned prompts, and a typed classifier) on the real household travel survey. Our results show that GAs with tuned prompts come close to the fitted models on the modal split of a simulated day without transport disruptions, but not trip by trip. GAs also react when unforeseen events occur. The recently released Jev-1.13 TypeSafe classifier gives surprisingly great results, showing that generating output text is not necessary. The results presented in this paper would be of interest to transport policy makers and service providers looking to apply generative agent-based modelling for improving urban mobility services.

Gossip Agents: Reputation through Opinion Dynamics over Evolving Networks

Yutong Wu, Arlei Silva

cs.MA

Sustained cooperation among self-interested learning agents is essential in many shared environments. Reputation systems are among the best-studied mechanisms for promoting such cooperation. Existing multi-agent reinforcement learning approaches, however, typically assume that reputation is either globally available to all agents or derived solely from an agent's own observations, leaving largely unexplored how reputational information propagates between agents. We study gossip, the exchange of information about third parties, as a mechanism for reputation diffusion over dynamic communication networks. Agents form private opinions from limited observations and exchange them with their neighbors following DeGroot opinion dynamics, while the communication network changes as agents interact and move. A tunable broadcast radius controls the reach of information, spanning private reputation, local gossip, and globally shared reputation. We study this framework in two \(n\)-player social dilemmas with distinct network dynamics: a Prisoner's Dilemma with randomly resampled interaction groups and Harvest, where communication neighborhoods emerge from agents' movements. Our results show that the reach of reputational information shapes cooperation in both environments. Moreover, in Harvest, reputation diffusion increases environmental rewards and leads to more peaceful and sustainable behavior.

Emergent Discrimination and Polarization in Multi-Agent Systems

Nestor Caticha, Jo\~ao Henrique Sant'Ana, Felippe Alves, Davi Bastos Costa, Mariana Mercucci, Juan P. Neirotti, Otavio Citton, Renato Vicente

cs.MA, cond-mat.dis-nn, physics.soc-ph

We study theoretically how discrimination and polarization can emerge in a population of learning agents. Each agent uses an adaptive linear decision layer to map a language-model embedding of an issue to a binary opinion and maintains an evolving estimate of how much it trusts every other agent. Through pairwise interactions, agents update both their decision weights and trust estimates using an online Bayesian rule derived from teacher-student learning over a noisy channel. In-context-learning experiments with large language models qualitatively reproduce the predicted opinion and trust updates. We then assign each agent one of two arbitrary group labels, A or B, and model group bias by allowing a fraction $f_b$ of agents to shift its perceived agreement with each message by a strength $b$, a bias steering prejudice, depending on whether the sender and receiver share a label. We use order parameters from statistical mechanics to connect these local dynamics to collective states and transitions. Starting from uniformly random initial conditions, simulations reveal a rich phase diagram in the $(b,f_b)$ plane, comprising four collective states: spin-glass frustrated out-group favoritism, nondiscriminatory polarization, discrimination with opinion alignment, and discrimination without opinion alignment. Which state emerges depends on both the strength and prevalence of the bias. These results show how simple local learning dynamics can generate qualitatively distinct forms of polarization and discrimination, underscoring the need for population-level safety analysis of multi-agent systems. They also suggest measurable order parameters for characterizing analogous collective states in human societies.

Thought Blocks: Non-Autoregressive Latent Communication for Multi-Agent Systems via Trajectory-to-State Compilation

Zhenhan Guan, Xiaolong Liu, Hengwei Ye, Zheng Tian

cs.MA

LLM-based multi-agent systems typically communicate through text, incurring repeated generation and re-encoding costs. Although latent communication removes explicit verbalization, constructing latent messages may still require autoregressive latent trajectories. We therefore distinguish message representation from message construction and ask whether agents can directly construct useful continuation states without executing the trajectories that normally produce them. We formulate this problem as trajectory-to-state compilation and introduce Thought Blocks, fixed-size blocks of latent key--value (KV) continuation memory constructed in one shot. Thought Blocks achieve a 2.5 times average latency speedup over autoregressive latent communication. Compared with text-based multi-agent communication, Thought Blocks reduce text tokens by 45.7% and autoregressive steps by 84.8%, achieving an 8.6 times average latency speedup. These results demonstrate that trajectory-to-state compilation enables efficient inter-agent latent communication without sequential latent rollouts at inference.

Existing post-training quantization (PTQ) methods provide them without retraining and, like those for ANNs, minimize numerical errors such as the mean squared error (MSE). In an SNN, however, a quantization error reaches later layers only through the spikes it flips. We find that errors of equal MSE can cause very different accuracy losses, whereas errors that flip the same fraction of spikes cause the same loss. We therefore propose SpikeQ, a spike-guided PTQ framework. For weights, we select the level of each weight by simulating the downstream neurons and minimizing their spike errors. For membrane potentials, most existing methods quantize the state after the reset, whereas neuromorphic chips compare the threshold with the membrane potential stored at a fixed bit width. Bayesian inference provides a computational account of such a localisation process; however, modelling efforts are largely limited to static listening scenarios. Instead, the free energy principle (FEP) describes perception, learning, and action as joint minimisation of variational free energy (VFE), providing a principled framework to account for dynamic conditions. We show this test is too permissive. Asymptotically, any sufficiently rich feature map, including a random one, admits a linear readout, so high probe accuracy is consistent with two very different geometries: a true linear world model, where the latent-aligned subspace dominates the representation, or a kernel machine, where the latents lie linearly inside a much larger curved manifold. This distinction matters because the curved structure orthogonal to the probe is relevant for out-of-distribution generalization, robustness, and compositional transfer. We validate them in a fully observed simulation where ground truth is known. Thus, forward decoding alone does not show that the probed factors align with the dominant geometry of the representation. Implemented as DPA4C-LR on the \texttt{nvalchemi} platform, the framework scales to 5.2 million atoms on four GPUs at approximately twice the computational cost of its short-range backbone. Trained on energies and forces, DPA4C-LR accurately infers BECs and reproduces the field-dependent infrared spectra of water. We further demonstrate its capabilities by recovering the characteristic slim polarization hysteresis of relaxor across Si/diamond interfaces with slab-corrected electrostatics. Code and data: https://github.com/manoharvellala/Hardeval The paper has 4 figure environments (two are images, two are the example-prompt panels) and 4 tables ------------------------------------------------------------------------------ Quantum kernels are among the most promising methods in quantum machine learning, providing a natural way to embed data into high-dimensional Hilbert spaces via quantum circuits and capturing non-linear relationships in data. Their learning behaviour is governed by the function class they represent, which is formally characterized by the structure of the associated Reproducing Kernel Hilbert Space (RKHS). However, a theoretical characterization of this function class, and of how it depends on the choice of feature map and its hyperparameters, is still lacking. In this work, we focus on the Instantaneous Quantum Polynomial (IQP) feature map and derive a Fourier representation of the associated RKHS generators as finite sums of trigonometric functions evaluated on polynomially transformed input variables, with a finite set of accessible frequencies determined by the circuit construction and scaled by the bandwidth hyperparameter. This representation identifies three bandwidth regimes with qualitatively distinct expressive properties from localised, through multimodal, to periodic. Numerical experiments on synthetic datasets with controlled periodic and multimodal structure, across data dimensions and feature-map configurations, reproduce the three regimes in both supervised classification and unsupervised anomaly detection, benchmarked against a classical Gaussian kernel. These results make the relationship between expressivity and data structure explicit for IQP kernels: expressive power translates into a practical benefit only when the accessible frequencies match with the structure of the data, which provides a criterion for selecting the bandwidth when the periodic or multimodal structure of data is known. arXiv:2610.23727 (*cross-listing*) Date: Fri, 16 Oct 2026 16:43:07 GMT (21791kb) Title: ECG-SDI: Single-lead Electrocardiogram Enables Continuous Sleep Depth Index Profiling and Scalable Digital Biomarkers for Sleep Health Authors: Donglin Xie, Qingshuo Zhao, Songchi Zhou, Haoyu Wang, Jingyu Wang, Yuxi Zhou, Thomas Penzel, Shenda Hong Categories: eess.SP cs.LG ------------------------------------------------------------------------------ Conservation laws reveal invariant structure in dynamical systems and constrain their evolution to lower-dimensional level sets. Locally, however, per the flow-box theorem, every $d$-dimensional system generically admits $d-1$ functionally independent conservation laws near a regular point even though, generically, only a few such laws persist globally. Data-driven methods typically learn conservation laws on a prescribed region of phase space from which training samples are drawn, leaving their behavior beyond that region unresolved. We formulate this local-to-global continuation problem and develop an {expanding-domain neural deflation} framework that tracks the number of independent conservation laws over nested domains. The method recovers the expected $d-1$ local invariants and detects their successive breakdown as the domain expands, relaxing to the expected global conservation law limit. It distinguishes the local invariants (associated with Frobenius integrability) from persistent global structure and shows that the domain scale over which additional laws survive reflects proximity to Liouville integrability. Pointwise losses can localize isolated obstructions, whereas in more complicated dynamics the breakdown of our loss functions is gradual and spatially diffuse. While versatile, the evidence is notoriously difficult to compute, even when the likelihood is available. In both likelihood-based and simulation-based inference, estimating $\mathcal{Z}$ provides an immediate solution to high-dimensional goodness-of-fit or model misspecification tests: using $\mathcal{Z}(x)$ as the test statistic induces a minimum-volume level-$\alpha$ test. Such tests are essential to assess model fidelity, which is necessary to trust simulation-based parameter inference. Existing learned approaches largely target Bayes factors, which only measure evidence ratios. Instead, we provide accurate, fast, and amortized estimates of the absolute evidence. We present ENFR: Evidence Networks with Flow-matched References. Building on the Evidence Network framework of Jeffrey & Wandelt (2024), the proposed method first learns Bayes factors with respect to a flow-matched reference, from which the absolute evidence is recovered. We show improvement in estimation of $\mathcal{Z}$ over the flow-based baseline in all applications considered and the best performance of all density estimation methods considered in our benchmarks. We furthermore introduce and provide the proof for an architecture that jointly learns the individual evidences of a set of models $\{\mathcal{M}_i\}$. We demonstrate the performance and scaling behavior of these approaches on data from a variety of scientific applications. arXiv:2610.23827 (*cross-listing*) Date: Fri, 16 Oct 2026 17:49:43 GMT (1350kb) Title: Quality-of-Transmission Estimation in Quantum Networks: Compute, Simulate, or Learn? Companion paper: Provable Compliance. Project page: https://chainit.com/white-papers/ ACM-class: K.6.5; K.4.4; D.4.6 ------------------------------------------------------------------------------ Multi-agent systems (MAS) comprise AI agents that collaborate or compete to perform complex, distributed tasks. Their dynamic interactions create opportunities for threat actors to target and impersonate other agents, intercept messages, inject malicious prompts, and exfiltrate sensitive data while simultaneously complicating the detection of anomalous behavior through conventional monitoring. This paper surveys threats and cryptographic challenges in agent-to-agent communication and presents a zero-trust architecture integrating identity, transport security, message integrity, and monitoring. The architecture establishes agent identities via decentralized identifiers and task-scoped verifiable credentials backed by a permissioned ledger, enforcing least-privilege authorization. For transport security, it provides mutually authenticated, crypto-agile TLS with hybrid post-quantum key establishment, and evaluates KEMTLS with pre-distributed public keys to reduce handshake overhead. Signed CBOR envelopes preserve payload integrity across multi-hop exchanges, while zero-knowledge proofs verify declared task constraints without exposing underlying inputs. Context-aware monitoring of inter-agent messages, tool activity, and execution telemetry informs the setting of risk-based trust thresholds and supports the detection of behavioral anomalies. Together, these mechanisms provide an integrated framework for securing and auditing agent communication, with implications for other agentic protocols and prospective 6G semantic communication.
